The Westminster
Confession of Faith

The Westminster Assembly, 1647

With the American amendments in their places, the Latin text in parallel, and the Shorter Catechism.

From Philip Schaff’s Creeds of Christendom, Volume III. The text is in the public domain.

The Westminster Confession of Faith, A.D. 1647

[The English text is taken from the second edition which appeared under the title, ’The Humble | Advice | of the | Assembly | of | Divines, | now by Authority of Parliament | sitting at Westminster, | concerning | a Confession of Faith: | with the Quotations and Texts of | Scripture annexed. Presented by them lately to both Houses of Parliament. | Printed at London; | and | reprinted at Edinburgh by Evan Tyler, Printer to | the Kings most Excellent Majestie. 1647.’ The spelling and punctuation are conformed to modern usage.

The changes of the American revision, which occur chiefly in Ch. XXIII., relating to the Civil Magistrate, and in Ch. XXXI., relating to Synods and Councils, are inserted in their proper places, and marked by italics. Minor changes are indicated in footnotes.

The Latin translation of the Westminster Confession and Catechisms by G. D. (see Preface) appeared first at Cambridge, 1656 (also 1659); at Edinburgh, 1694, etc.; and at Glasgow, 1660), under the title, ’Confessio Fidei | in Conventu theologorum authoritate | Parliamenti Anglicani indicto | Elaborata; | eidem Parliamento postmodum | Exhibita; | Quin et ab eodem, deindeque ab Ecclesia Scoticana | Cognita et Approbata; | und cum | Catechismo | duplici, Majori, Minorique; | E Sermone Anglicano summa cum fide | in Litinum versa. Cantabrigiæ: excudebat Johannes Field, celeberrimæ Academiæ typographus.’]

Confession of Faith.

Confessio Fidei.

Chapter I. Of the Holy Scripture

I. Although the light of nature, and the works of creation and providence, do so far manifest the goodness, wisdom, and power of God, as to leave men inexcusable;1 yet are they not sufficient to give that knowledge of God, and of his will, which is necessary unto salvation;2 therefore it pleased the Lord, at sundry times, and in divers manners, to reveal himself, and to declare that his will unto his Church;3 and afterwards, for the better preserving and propagating of the truth, and for the more sure establishment and comfort of the Church against the corruption of the flesh, and the malice

I. Quanquam naturæ lumen, operaque Dei cum Creationis tum Providentiæ, bonitatem ejus, sapientiam, potentiamque eo usque manifestant, ut homines vel inde reddantur inexcusabiles:4 eam tamen Dei, voluntatisque divinæ cognitionem, quæ porro est ad salutem necessaria, nequeunt nobis ingenerare.5 Quocirca Domino complacitum est, variis quidem modis vicibusque Ecclesiæ suæ semetipsum revelare, suamque hanc voluntatem patefacere;6 sed et eandem omnem postea literis consignare, quo et veritati suæ tam conservandæ quam propagandæ melius consuleret, nec Ecclesia sua contra carnis corruptelam, contra malitiam mundi

of Satan and of the world, to commit the same wholly unto writing;7 which maketh the holy Scripture to be most necessary;8 those former ways of God’s revealing his will unto his people being now ceased.9

Satanæque, præsidio foret ac solatio destituta.10 Unde factum est, ut, postquam pristini illi modi, quibus olim populo suo Deus voluntatem suam revelabat, jam desiverint,11 Scriptura Sacra sit maxime necessaria.12

II. Under the name of holy Scripture, or the Word of God written, are now contained all the Books of the Old and New Testament, which are these:

II. Sacræ Scripturæ, nomine, seu Verbi Dei scripti continentur hodie omnes illi libri tam Veteris quam Novi Instrumenti,13 nempe quorum inferius subsequuntur nomina.

All which are given by inspiration of God, to be the rule of faith and life.14

Qui omnes divina inspiratione dati sunt in Fidei vitæque regulam.15

III. The books commonly called Apocrypha, not being of divine inspiration, are no part of the Canon of the Scripture; and therefore are of no authority in the Church of God, nor to be any otherwise approved, or made use of, than other human writings.16

III. Libri Apocryphi, vulgo dicti, quum non fuerint divinitus inspirati, Canonem Scripturæ nullatenus constituunt; proindeque nullam aliam authoritatem obtinere debent in Ecclesia Dei, nec aliter quam alia humana scripta, sunt aut approbandi aut adhibendi.17

IV. The authority of the holy Scripture, for which it ought to be believed and obeyed, dependeth not upon the testimony of any man or church, but wholly upon God (who is truth itself), the Author thereof; and therefore it is to be received, because it is the Word of God.18

IV. Authoritas Scripturæ sacræ propter quam ei debetur fides et observantia, non ab ullius aut hominis aut Ecclesiæ pendet testimonio, sed a solo ejus authore Deo, qui est ipsa veritas: eoque est a nobis recipienda, quoniam est Verbum Dei.19

V. We may be moved and induced by the testimony of the

V. Testimonium Ecclesiæ efficere quidem potest ut de Scriptura sacra

Church to an high and reverent esteem of20 the holy Scripture;21 and the heavenliness of the matter, the efficacy of the doctrine, the majesty of the style, the consent of all the parts, the scope of the whole (which is to give all glory to God), the full discovery it makes of the only way of man’s salvation, the many other incomparable excellencies, and the entire perfection thereof, are arguments whereby it doth abundantly evidence itself to be the Word of God; yet, notwithstanding, our full persuasion and assurance of the infallible truth, and divine authority thereof, is from the inward work of the Holy Spirit, bearing witness by and with the Word in our hearts.22

quam honorifice sentiamus;23 materies insuper ejus cœlestis, doctrinæ vis et efficacia, styli majestas, partium omnium consensus, totiusque scopus (ut Deo nempe omnis gloria tribuatur), plena denique quam exhibet unicæ ad salutem viæ commonstratio, præter alias ejus virtutes incomparabiles, et perfectionem summam, argumenta sunt quibus abunde se Verbum Dei et luculenter probat; nihilominus tamen plena persuasio et certitudo de ejus tam infallibili veritate, quam authoritate divina non aliunde nascitur quam ab interna operatione Spiritus Sancti, per verbum et cum verbo ipso in cordibus nostris testificantis.24

VI. The whole counsel of God, concerning all things necessary for his own glory, man’s salvation, faith, and life, is either expressly set down in Scripture, or by good and necessary consequence may be deduced from Scripture: unto which nothing at any time is to be added, whether by new revelations of the Spirit, or traditions of men.25 Nevertheless we acknowledge the inward illumination of the Spirit of God to be necessary for the saving

VI. Consilium Dei universum de omnibus quæ ad suam ipsius gloriam, quæque ad hominum salutem, fidem, vitamque sunt necessaria, aut expresse in Scriptura continetur, aut consequentia bona et necessaria derivari potest a Scriptura; cui nihil deinceps addendum est, seu novis a spiritu revelationibus, sive traditionibus hominum.26 Internam nihilominus illuminationem Spiritus Dei ad salutarem eorum perceptionem, quæ in Verbo Dei

understanding of such things as are revealed in the Word;27 and that there are some circumstances concerning the worship of God, and government of the Church, common to human actions and societies, which are to be ordered by the light of nature and Christian prudence, according to the general rules of the Word, which are always to be observed.28

revelantur, agnoscimus esse necessariam:29 quin etiam nonnullas esse circumstantias cultum Dei spectantes et Ecclesiæ regimen, iis cum humanis actionibus et societatibus communes, quæ naturali lumine ac prudentia Christiana secundum generales verbi regulas (perpetuo quidem illas observandas) sunt regulandæ.30

VII. All things in Scripture are not alike plain in themselves, nor alike clear unto all;31 yet those things which are necessary to be known, believed, and observed, for salvation, are so clearly propounded and opened in some place of Scripture or other, that not only the learned, but the unlearned, in a due use of the ordinary means, may attain unto a sufficient understanding of them.32

VII. Quæ in Scriptura continentur non sunt omnia æque aut in se perspicua, aut omnibus hominibus evidentia,33 ea tamen omnia quæ ad salutem necessaria sunt cognitu, creditu, observatu, adeo perspicue, alicubi saltem in Scriptura, proponuntur et explicantur, ut eorum non docti solum, verum indocti etiam ordinariorum debito usu mediorum, sufficientem assequi possint intelligentiam.34

VIII. The Old Testament in Hebrew (which was the native language of the people of God of old), and the New Testament in Greek (which at the time of the writing of it was most generally known to the nations), being immediately inspired by God, and by his singular care and providence kept pure in all ages, are therefore authentical;35 so as in all controversies

VIII. Instrumentum Vetus Hebræa lingua (antiqua Dei populo nempe vernacula) Novum autem Græca (ut quæ apud Gentes maxime omnium tunc temporis, quum scriberetur illud, obtinuerat), immediate a Deo inspirata, ejusque cura et Providentia singulari per omnia huc usque secula pura et intaminata custodita, ea propter sunt authentica.36 Adeo sane ut ad

of religion the Church is finally to appeal unto them.37 But because these original tongues are not known to all the people of God who have right unto, and interest in the Scriptures, and are commanded, in the fear of God, to read and search them,38 therefore they are to be translated into the vulgar language of every nation unto which they come,39 that the Word of God dwelling plentifully in all, they may worship him in an acceptable manner,40 and, through patience and comfort of the Scriptures, may have hope.41

illa ultimo in omnibus de religione controversiis Ecclesia debeat appellare.42 Quoniam autem Originales istæ linguæ non sunt toti Dei populo intellectæ (Quorum tamen et jus est ut scripturas habeant, et interest plurimum, quique eas in timore Dei legere jubentur et perscrutari)43 proinde sunt in vulgarem cujusque Gentis, ad quam pervenerint linguam transferendæ,44 ut omnes, verbo Dei opulenter in ipsis habitante, Deum grato acceptoque modo colant,45 et per patientiam ac consolationem Scripturarum spem habeant.46

IX. The infallible rule of interpretation of Scripture is the Scripture itself; and therefore, when there is a question about the true and full sense of any Scripture (which is not manifold, but one), it must47 be searched and known by other places that speak more clearly.48

IX. Infallibilis Scripturam interpretandi regula est Scriptura ipsa. Quoties igitur cunque oritur quæstio de. vero plenoque Scripturæ cujusvis sensu (unicus ille est non multiplex), ex aliis locis, qui apertius loquuntur, est indagandus et cognoscendus.49

X. The Supreme Judge, by which all controversies of religion are to be determined, and all decrees of councils, opinions of ancient writers, doctrines of men, and private spirits, are to be examined, and in whose sentence we are to rest, can

X. Supremus judex, a quo omnes de religione controversiæ sunt determinandæ, omnia Conciliorum decreta, opiniones Scriptorum Veterum, doctrinæ denique hominum, et privati quicunque Spiritus sunt examinandi, cujusque sententia tenemur

be no other but the Holy Spirit speaking in the Scripture.50

nemur acquiescere, nullus alius esse potest, præter Spiritum Sanctum in Scriptura pronunciantem.51

Chapter II. Of God, and of the Holy Trinity

I. There is but one only52 living and true God,53 who is infinite in being and perfection,54 a most pure spirit,55 invisible,56 without body, parts,57 or passions,58 immutable,59 immense,60 eternal,61 incomprehensible,62 almighty,63 most wise,64 most holy,65 most free,66 most absolute,67 working all things according to the counsel of his own immutable and most righteous will,68 for his own glory;69 most loving,70 gracious, merciful, longsuffering, abundant in goodness and truth, forgiving iniquity, transgression, and sin;71 the rewarder of them that diligently seek him;72 and withal most just and terrible in his judgments;73

I. Unus est unicusque,74 vivens ille et verus Deus:75 qui idem est essentia et perfectione infinitus,76 Spiritus purissimus,77 invisibilis,78 sine corpore, sine partibus,79 sine passionibus,80 immutabilis,81 immensus,82 æternus,83 incomprehensibilis,84 omnipotens,85 summe sapiens,86 summe sanctus,87 liberrimus,88 maxime absolutus;89 operans omnia secundum consilium immutabilis suæ ac justissimæ voluntatis,90 ad suam ipsius gloriam;91 idemque summa benignitate,92 gratia, misericordia, et longanimitate; bonitate abundans et veritate; condonans iniquitatem, transgressionem et peccatum;93 studiose quærentium ipsum remunerator;94 sed et in judiciis suis justissimus idem ac tremendus maxime;95

hating all sin,96 and who will by no means clear the guilty. .97

peccatum omne perosus,98 et qui sontem nullo unquam absolvet modo.99

II. God hath all life,100 glory,101 goodness,102 blessedness,103 in and of himself; and is alone in and unto himself allsufficient, not standing in need of any creatures which he hath made,104 nor deriving any glory from them,105 but only manifesting his own glory in, by, unto, and upon them: he is the alone foundation of all being, of whom, through whom, and to whom are all things;106 and hath most sovereign dominion over them, to do by them, for them, or upon them whatsoever himself pleaseth.107 In his sight all things are open and manifest;108 his knowledge is infinite, infallible, and independent upon the creature;109 so as nothing is to him contingent or uncertain.110 He is most holy in all his counsels, in all his works, and in all his commands.111 To him is due from angels and men, and every other creature, whatsoever worship, service, or obedience, he is pleased to require of them.112

II. Omnem vitam,113 omnem gloriam,114 bonitatem,115 beatitudinemque116 omnem in sese habet et a seipso Deus; qui solus in se sibique est ad omnia sufficiens; creaturarum, quas ipse condidit, nullius egens,117 nec gloriam ab eis derivans ullam,118 verum in iis, per eas, iis ipsis, ac super eas propriam ipsius gloriam tantummodo manifestans. Is omnis entitatis fans est unicus, a quo, per quem et ad quem omnia;119 summumque in ea dominium habet, ac per illa, pro illis, in illa pro suo arbitrio quidlibet agendi potestatem.120 In conspectu ejus aperta sunt omnia ac manifesta;121 scientia ejus infinita est, infallibilis, atque a creatura independens,122 adeo ut illi contingens incertumve nihil sit;123 in omnibus ejus consiliis, operibus et mandatis est sanctissimus.124 Quicquid cultus, quicquid officii, quicquid obsequii ab Angelis illi, ab hominibus, aut a quavis creatura exigere placet, id illi omne jure optimo debetur.125

III. In the unity of the Godhead

III. In Deitatis unitate personæ

head there be three persons, of one substance, power, and eternity: God the Father, God the Son, and God the Holy Ghost.126 The Father is of none, neither begotten nor proceeding; the Son is eternally begotten of the Father;127 the Holy Ghost eternally proceeding from the Father and the Son.128

tres sunt unius ejusdemque essentiæ, potential ac æternitatis; Deus Pater, Deus Filius, ac Deus Spiritus Sanctus.129 Pater quidem a nullo est, nec genitus nempe nec procedens: Filius autem a Patre est æterne genitus:130 Spiritus autem Sanctus æterne procedens a Patre Filioque.131

Chapter III. Of God’s Eternal Decree

132

I. God from all eternity did, by the most wise and holy counsel of his own will, freely and unchangeably ordain whatsoever comes to pass;133 yet so as thereby neither is God the author of sin,134 nor is violence offered to the will of the creatures, nor is the liberty or contingency of second causes taken away, but rather established.135

I. Deus, e sapientissimo sanctissimoque consilio voluntatis suæ, libere ac immutabiliter, quicquid unquam evenit, ab omni æterno ordinavit;136 ita tamen, id inde nec author peccati evadat Deus,137 nec voluntati creaturarum sit vis illata, neque libertas aut contingentia causarum secundarum ablata sit, verum potius stabilita.138

II. Although God knows whatsoever may or can come to pass upon all supposed conditions,139 yet hath he not decreed any thing because he foresaw it as future, or as that which would come to pass upon such conditions.140

II. Quamvis omnia cognoscat Deus, quæ suppositis quibusvis conditionibus sunt eventu possibilia;141 non tamen ideo quicquam decrevit quoniam illud præviderat aut futurum, aut positis talibus conditionibus eventurum.142

III. By the decree of God, for the manifestation of his glory,

III. Deus, quo gloriam suam manifestaret, nonnullos hominum

some men and angels143 are predestinated unto everlasting life, and others foreordained to everlasting death.144

ac Angelorum145 decreto suo ad æternam vitam prædestinavit, alios autem ad mortem æternam præordinavit.146

IV. These angels and men, thus predestinated and foreordained, are particularly and unchangeably designed; and their number is so certain and definite that it can not be either increased or diminished.147

IV. prædestinati illi et præordinati homines Angelique, particulariter sunt ac immutabiliter designati, certusque illorum est ac definitus numerus, adeo ut nec augeri possit nec imminui.148

V. Those of mankind that are predestinated unto life, God, before the foundation of the world was laid, according to his eternal and immutable purpose, and the secret counsel and good pleasure of his will, hath chosen in Christ, unto everlasting glory,149 out of his mere free grace and love, without any foresight of faith or good works, or perseverance in either of them, or any other thing in the creature, as conditions, or causes moving him thereunto;150 and all to the praise of his glorious grace.151

V. Qui ex humano genere sunt ad vitam prædestinati, illos Deus ante jacta mundi fundamenta, secundum æternum suum ac immutabile propositum, secretumque voluntatis suæ consilium et beneplacitum, elegit in Christo ad æternam gloriam,152 idque ex amore suo et gratia mere gratuita; nec fide, nec bonis operibus, nec in his illave perseverantia, sed neque ulla alia re in creatura, prævisis, ipsum tanquam causis aut conditionibus ad id moventibus;153 quo totum nempe in laudem cederet gloriosæ suæ gratiæ.154

VI. As God hath appointed the elect unto glory, so hath he, by the eternal and most free purpose of his will, foreordained all the means thereunto.155 Wherefore they who are elected, being fallen in

VI. Quemadmodum autem Deus electos ad gloriam destinavit, sic omnia etiam quibus illam consequantur media præordinavit, voluntatis suæ proposito æterno simul et liberrimo.156 Quapropter electi, postquam

Adam, are redeemed by Christ,157 are effectually called unto faith in Christ by his Spirit working in due season; are justified, adopted, sanctified,158 and kept by his power through faith unto salvation.159 Neither are any other redeemed by Christ, effectually called, justified, adopted, sanctified, and saved, but the elect only.160

lapsi essent in Adamo, a Christo sunt redempti;161 per Spiritum ejus opportuno tempore operantem, ad fidem in Christum vocantur efficaciter; justificantur, sanctificantur,162 et potentia ipsius per fidem custodiuntur ad salutem.163 Nec alii quivis a Christo redimuntur, vocantur efficaciter justificantur, adoptantur, sanctificantur et salvantur, præter electos solos.164

VII. The rest of mankind God was pleased, according to the unsearchable counsel of his own will, whereby he extendeth or withholdeth mercy as he pleaseth, for the glory of his sovereign power over his creatures, to pass by, and to ordain them to dishonor and wrath for their sin, to the praise of his glorious justice.165

VII. Reliquos humani generis Deo placuit secundum consilium voluntatis suæ inscrutabile {quo misericordiam pro libitu exhibet abstinetve) in gloriam supremæ suæ in creaturas potestatis, præterire; eosque ordinare ad ignominiam et iram pro peccatis suis, ad laudem justitiæ suæ gloriosæ.166

VIII. The doctrine of this high mystery of predestination is to be handled with special prudence and care,167 that men attending the will of God revealed in his Word, and yielding obedience thereunto, may, from the certainty of their effectual vocation, be assured of their eternal election.168 So shall this doctrine afford matter of praise, reverence, and admiration of God;169 and of humility, diligence, and abundant consolation

VIII. Doctrina de sublimi hoc prædestinationis mysterio non sine summa cura et prudentia tractari debet,170 quo nimirum homines, dum voluntati Dei in verbo ejus revelatæ advertant animos, eique debitam exhibeant obedientiam, de efficaci sua vocatione certiores facti, ad æternæ suæ electionis assurgere possint certitudinem.171 Ita demum suppeditabit hæc doctrina laudandi, reverendi, admirandique Deum argumentum,172 quin etiam humilitatis,

to all that sincerely obey the gospel.173

diligentiæ et consolationis copiosæ omnibus sincere obedientibus evangelio.174

Chapter IV. Of Creation

I. It pleased God the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost,175 for the manifestation of the glory of his eternal power, wisdom, and goodness,176 in the beginning, to create or make of nothing the world, and all things therein, whether visible or invisible, in the space of six days, and all very good.177

I. Deo, Patri, Filio et Spiritui sancto, complacitum est,178 quo æternæ suæ cum potentiæ tum sapientiæ bonitatisque gloriam manifestaret,179 mundum hunc, et quæ in eo continentur universa tam visibilla quam invisibilia, in principio intra sex dierum spatium creare, seu ex nihilo condere, atque omnia quidem bona valde.180

II. After God had made all other creatures, he created man, male and female,181 with reasonable and immortal souls,182 endued with knowledge, righteousness, and true holiness, after his own image,183 having the law of God written in their hearts,184 and power to fulfill it;185 and yet under a possibility of transgressing, being left to the liberty of their own will, which was subject unto change.186 Beside this law written in their hearts, they received a command not to eat of the tree of the knowledge of good

II. Postquam omnes alias creaturas condidisset Deus, creavit hominem marem et fœminam,187 animabus inditis rationalibus ac immortalibus,188 imbutos cognitione, justitia, veraque sanctitate, ad suam ipsius imaginem,189 habentes in cordibus suis inscriptam Divinam legem,190 simul et eandem implendi vires;191 non tamen sine quadam violandi possibilitate; libertati siquidem permissi erant voluntatis suæ haud immutabilis.192 Præter autem hanc in cordibus eorum inscriptam legem de non comedendo ex arbore scientiæ boni malique

and evil; which while they kept they were happy in their communion with God,193 and had dominion over the creatures.194

mandatum insuper acceperunt; quod certe quam diu observabant, communione Dei beati erant,195 dominiumque habebant in creaturas.196

Chapter V. Of Providence

God, the great Creator of all things, doth uphold,197 direct, dispose, and govern all creatures, actions, and things,198 from the greatest even to the least,199 by his most wise and holy providence,200 according to his infallible foreknowledge201 and the free and immutable counsel of his own will,202 to the praise of the glory of his wisdom, power, justice, goodness, and mercy.203

I. Magnus ille rerum omnium creator Deus sapientissima sua et sanctissima simul providentia204 creaturas, actiones, resque205 a maximis usque ad minimas206 universas sustentat,207 dirigit, ordinat, gubernatque secundum infallibilem suam præscientiam,208 et voluntatis suæ consilium liberum ac immutabile,209 ad laudem gloriæ sapientiæ suæ, potentiæ, justitiæ, bonitatis, ac misericordiæ.210

II. Although in relation to the foreknowledge and decree of God, the first cause, all things come to pass immutably and infallibly,211 yet by the same providence he ordereth them to fall out, according to the nature of second causes, either necessarily, freely, or contingently.212

II. Quamvis respectu præscientiæ ac decreti Dei (causæ primæ) omnia immutabiliter atque infallibiliter eveniant,213 per eandem tamen ille providentiam eadem ordinat evenire necessario, libere, aut contingenter, pro natura causarum secundarum.214

III. God, in his ordinary providence, maketh use of means,215 yet is free to work without,216

III. Deus in providentia sua ordinaria mediis utitur,217 iis tamen non astringitur, quo minus absque eis,218

above,219 and against them, at his pleasure.220

supra221 aut etiam contra ea pro arbitrio suo operetur.222

IV. The almighty power, unsearchable wisdom, and infinite goodness of God so far manifest themselves in his providence that it extendeth itself even to the first fall, and all other sins of angels and men,223 and that not by a bare permission,224 but such as hath joined with it a most wise and powerful bounding,225 and otherwise ordering and governing of them, in a manifold dispensation, to his own holy ends;226 yet so as the sinfulness thereof proceedeth only from the creature, and not from God; who, being most holy and righteous, neither is nor can be the author or approver of sin.227

IV. Omnipotentem Dei potentiam, sapientiam inscrutabilem, bonitatemque infinitam providentia ejus eo usque manifestat, ut vel ad primum lapsum, omniaque reliqua peccata, seu hominum sint sive angelorum, se extendat;228 neque id quidem permissione nuda,229 verum cui conjuncta est sapientissima potentissimaque eorum limitatio,230 ac aliusmodi ad sanctos sibi propositos fines dispensatione multiplici ordinatio et gubernatio;231 ita tamen ut omnis eorum vitiositas a sola proveniat creatura, a Deo neutiquam, qui sanctissimus quum sit justissimusque neque est, nec esse quidem potest peccati autor aut approbator.232

V. The most wise, righteous, and gracious God doth oftentimes leave for a season his own children to manifold temptations and the corruption of their own hearts, to chastise them for their former sins, or to discover unto them the hidden strength of corruption and deceitfulness of their hearts, that they may be humbled;233 and to raise them to a more close and constant

V. Sapientissimus, justissimus, et gratiosissimus idem Deus, sæpenumero filios suos tentationibus multifariis, suorumque cordium corruptioni ad tempus permittit; quo ob admissa prius peccata castiget eos, vel corruptionis iis detegat vim occultam, cordiumque suorum fraudulentiam ut humilientur;234 quoque eos excitet ad strictam magis et constantem a seipso proferendis suppetiis

dependence for their support unto235 himself, and to make them more watchful against all future occasions of sin, and for sundry other just and holy ends.236

dependentiam; Quo denique adversus onmes occasiones peccati de futuro reddat cautiores. Sed et ob alios etiam varios fines, justos sanctosque sibi propositos.237

VI. As for those wicked and ungodly men whom God, as a righteous judge, for former sins, doth blind and harden,238 from them he not only withholdeth his grace, whereby they might have been enlightened in their understandings and wrought upon in their hearts,239 but sometimes also withdraweth the gifts which they had,240 and exposeth them to such objects as their corruption makes occasion of sin;241 and withal, gives them over to their own lusts, the temptations of the world, and the power of Satan;242 whereby it comes to pass that they harden themselves, even under those means which God useth for the softening of others.243

VI. Quod scelestos illos spectat impiosque homines, quos Deus, ut justus judex, ob peccata præcedentia excæcat induratque;244 eis ille non solum gratiam suam non impertit, qua ipsis cum illuminari intellectus, tum affici corda potuissent;245 sed interdum subtrahit eis quibus imbuti erant dona,246 et ipsos exponit illiusmodi objectis, unde corruptio eorum arripit sibi peccandi occasiones;247 simulque tradit eos suis ipsorum concupiscentiis et tentationibus mundi, et potestati Satanæ;248 ex quo fit ut seipsos ipsi indurent, et quidem sub iisdem mediis quibus utitur Deus ad alios emolliendos.249

VII. As the providence of God doth, in general, reach to all creatures, so, after a most special manner, it taketh care of his Church, and disposeth all things to the good thereof.250

VII. Providentia Dei sicut ad omnes creaturas universali modo se extendit; ita modo plane peculiari Ecclesiæ suæ curam gerit, ac in ejus bonum disponit universa.251

Chapter VI. Of the Fall of Man, of Sin, and of the Punishment thereof

I. Our first parents, being seduced by the subtilty and temptation of Satan, sinned in eating the forbidden fruit.252 This their sin God was pleased, according to his wise and holy counsel, to permit, having purposed to order it to his own glory.253

I. Primi parentes, Satanæ subtilitate ac tentatione seducti, fructus vetiti esu peccaverunt.254 Hoc eorum peccatum secundum sapiens suum sanctumque consilium Deo placuit permittere, non sine proposito illud ad suam ipsius gloriam ordinandi.255

II. By this sin they fell from their original righteousness and communion with God,256 and so became dead in sin,257 and wholly defiled in all the faculties and parts of soul and body.258

II. Hoc illi peccato, justitia sua originali et communione cum Deo exciderunt;259 itaque facti sunt in peccato mortui,260 atque in omnibus facultatibus ac partibus animæ corporisque penitus contaminati.261

III. They being the root of all mankind,262 the guilt of this sin was imputed, and the same death in sin and corrupted nature conveyed to all their posterity descending from them by ordinary generation.263

III. Quumque illi fuerint radix totius humani264 generis, hujusce peccati reatus fuit imputatus, eademque in peccato mors ac natura corrupta propagata, omnibus illorum posteris, quotquot ab iis ordinaria quidem generatione procreantur.265

IV. From this original corruption, whereby we are utterly indisposed, disabled, and made opposite to all good,266 and wholly inclined to all evil,267 do proceed all actual transgressions.268

IV. Ab hac originali labe (qua ad omne bonum facti sumus inhabiles prorsus ac impotentes, eique plane oppositi,269 ad malum autem omne proclives penitus)270 proveniunt omnia peccata actualia.271

V. This corruption of nature,

V. Hæc naturæ corruptio durante

during this life, doth remain in those that are regenerated;272 and although it be through Christ pardoned and mortified, yet both itself and all the motions thereof are truly and properly sin.273

hac vita manet etiam in regenitis;274 et quamvis per Christum et condonata sit et mortificata; nihilo minus tam ipsa, quam ejus motus universi vere sunt ac proprie peccata.275

VI. Every sin, both original and actual, being a transgression of the righteous law of God, and contrary thereunto,276 doth, in its own nature, bring guilt upon the sinner,277 whereby he is bound over to the wrath of God278 and curse of the law,279 and so made subject to death,280 with all miseries spiritual,281 temporal,282 and eternal.283

VI. Peccatum omne cum originale tum actuale, quum justæ Dei legis transgressio sit eique contraria,284 peccatori suapte natura reatum infert,285 quo ad iram Dei,286 ac maledictionem legis287 subeundam obligatur, adeoque redditur obnoxius morti288 simul et miseriis omnibus spiritualibus,289 temporalibus,290 ac æternis.291

Chapter VII. Of God’s Covenant with Man

I. The distance between God and the creature is so great that although reasonable creatures do owe obedience unto him as their Creator, yet they could never have any fruition of him as their blessedness and reward but by some voluntary condescension on God’s part, which he hath been pleased to express by way of covenant.292

I. Tanta est inter deum et creaturam distantia, ut licet creaturæ rationales obedientiam illi ut creatori suo debeant, nullam tamen fruitionem ejus tanquam suæ beatitudinis ac præmii habere unquam potuissent, ni voluntaria fuisset aliqua ex parte Dei condescentio; quam ipsi exprimere placuit icto fœdere.293

II. The first covenant made with

II. Primum fœdus cum hominibus

man was a covenant of works,294 wherein life was promised to Adam, and in him to his posterity,295 upon condition of perfect and personal obedience.296

initum erat fœdus operum,297 quo vita Adamo promissa erat, ejusque in eo posteris,298 sub conditione obedientiæ perfectæ ac personalis.299

III. Man by his fall having made himself incapable of life by that covenant, the Lord was pleased to make a second,300 commonly called the covenant of grace: wherein he freely offered unto sinners life and salvation by Jesus Christ, requiring of them faith in him that they may be saved,301 and promising to give unto all those that are ordained unto life his Holy Spirit, to make them willing and able to believe.302

III. Quum autem homo lapsu suo omnem sibi præstruxisset ad vitam aditum per illud fœdus, complacuit Domino secundum inire,303 quod vulgo dicimus Fœdus Gratiæ; in quo peccatoribus offert gratuito vitam ac salutem per Jesum Christum, fidem in illum ab iis requirens ut salventur;304 promittensque omnibus qui ad vitam ordinantur se spiritum suum sanctum daturum, qui in illis operetur credendi cum voluntatem tum potentiam.305

IV. This covenant of grace is frequently set forth in the Scripture by the name of a testament, in reference to the death of Jesus Christ the testator, and to the everlasting inheritance, with all things belonging to it, therein bequeathed.306

IV. Hoc fœdus Gratiæ in Scriptura sæpe nomine Testamenti indigitatur, respectu nimirum mortis Testatoris Jesu Christi, æternæque illius hæreditatis, quam is una cum omnibus eam spectantibus inibi legabat.307

V. This covenant was differently administered in the time of the law and in the time of the gospel:308 under the law it was administered by promises, prophecies,

V. Hoc fœdus sub Lege atque sub Evangelio administratum est modo alio atque alio.309 Sub Lege quidem per promissiones, prophetias et sacrificia, per circumcisionem, agnum

sacrifices, circumcision, the paschal lamb, and other types and ordinances delivered to the people of the Jews, all fore-signifying Christ to come,310 which were for that time sufficient and efficacious, through the operation of the Spirit, to instruct and build up the elect in faith in the promised Messiah,311 by whom they had full remission of sins and eternal salvation; and is called the Old Testament.312

pascalem, aliosque typos ac instituta populo Judaico tradita, quæ omnia Venturum Christum præsignificabant;313 erantque pro ratione illorum temporum sufficientia, et per operationem spiritus efficacia ad electos instruendum ac ædificandum in fide in promissum Messiam,314 per quem plenum peccatorum remissionem et salutem æternam sunt consecuti; diciturque Vetus Testamentum.315

VI. Under the gospel, when Christ the substance316 was exhibited, the ordinances in which this covenant is dispensed are the preaching of the word and the administration of the sacraments of Baptism and the Lord’s Supper;317 which, though fewer in number, and administered with more simplicity aud less outward glory, yet in them it is held forth in more fullness, evidence, and spiritual efficacy,318 to all nations, both Jews and Gentiles;319 and is called the New Testament.320 There are not, therefore, two covenants of grace differing in substance, but one and the same under various dispensations.321

VI. Sub evangelio autem, exhibito jam Christo, substantia322 scilicet ac antitypo, præscriptæ rationes in quibus hoc fœdus dispensatur, sunt prædicatio verbi, et administratio sacramentorum, baptismi nempe ac cœnæ Dominicæ;323 in quibus quidem utut numero paucioribus, iisque simplicius ac minore cum externa gloria administratis, cum majore tamen plenitudine, evidentia, et efficacia spirituali324 populis cunctis tam Judæis quam Gentibus325 exhibetur; Diciturque Novum Testamentum.326 Non sunt ergo duo fœdera gratiæ, re atque natura discrepantia; sed unum idemque, licet non uno modo dispensatum.327.

Chapter VIII. Of Christ the Mediator

I. It pleased God, in his eternal purpose, to choose and ordain the Lord Jesus, his only-begotten Son, to be the Mediator between God and man,328 the Prophet,329 Priest,330 and King;331 the Head and Saviour of his Church,332 the Heir of all things,333 and Judge of the world;334 whom he did, from all eternity, give a people to be his seed,335 and to be by him in time redeemed, called, justified, sanctified, and glorified.336

I. Complacitum est Deo Filium ejus unigenitum Dominum Jesum in æterno suo proposito eligere atque ordinare ut Mediator esset inter Deum et hominem,337 Propheta,338 Sacerdos,339 et Rex,340 caput idem et salvator Ecclesiæ suæ;341 rerum omnium hæres,342 Mundique Judex;343 cui ab æterno populum dedit futurum illi in semen,344 ac per illum stato tempore redimendum, vocandum, justificandum, sanctificandum ac glorificandum.345

II. The Son of God, the second person in the Trinity, being very and eternal God, of one substance, and equal with the Father did, when the fullness of time was come, fake upon him man’s nature,346 with all the essential properties and common infirmities thereof, yet without sin:347 being conceived by the power of the Holy Ghost in the womb of the Virgin Mary, of her substance.348 So that two whole, perfect, and distinct natures, the Godhead and the manhood, were inseparably joined together

II. Filius Dei persona secunda in Trinitate, verus nempe idem æternusque Deus, substantiæ cum Patre unius ejusdemque, eique coæqualis, cum advenerat temporis plenitudo, assumpsit naturam humanam,349 una cum omnibus ejus proprietatibus essentialibus, communibusque infirmitatibus, immunem tamen a peccato,350 conceptus scilicet in utero eque substantia Mariæ Virginis,351 virtute Spiritus Sancti. Adeo sane ut naturæ duæ, integræ, perfectæ, distinctæque Deitas ac humanitas in una eademque

in one person, without conversion, composition, or confusion.352 Which person is very God and very man, yet one Christ, the only mediator between God and man.353

persona indissolubili nexu conjunctæ fuerint, sine conversione, compositione, aut confusione.354 Quæ quidem persona vere Deus est ac vere homo, unus tamen Christus, unicus inter Deum et hominem Mediator.355

III. The Lord Jesus, in his human nature thus united to the divine, was sanctified and anointed with the Holy Spirit above measure;356 having in him all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge,357 in whom it pleased the Father that all fullness should dwell;358 to the end that, being holy, harmless, undefiled, and full of grace and truth,359 he might be thoroughly furnished to execute the office of a mediator and surety.360 Which office he took not unto himself, but was thereunto called by his Father,361 who put all power and judgment into his hand, and gave him commandment to execute the same.362

III. Dominus Jesus in humana sua natura divinæ hunc modum conjuncta sanctificatus est, ac Spiritu sancto supra mensuram unctus,363 in se habens omnes sapientiæ notitiæqum thesauros;364 in quo Patri visum est ut omnis plenitudo inhabitaret,365 atque eo quidem fine ut sanctus, innocuus, intaminatus, plenusque gratiæ ac veritatis existens,366 ad Mediatoris Vadisque munus exequendum perfecte esset instructus.367 Quod ille officium non arripuit sibi, verum a Patre erat ad id vocatus,368 qui omnem ei potestatem ac judicium in manus dedit, und cum mandato exercendi.369

IV. This office the Lord Jesus did most willingly undertake,370 which, that he might discharge, he was made under the law,371 and did perfectly fulfill it;372 endured most grievous torments immediately in his soul,373

IV. Hoc munus promtissima voluntate in se suscepit Dominus Jesus,374 quod ut expleret factus est sub Lege,375 eam perfecte implevit,376 immediate in anima,377 sua gravissimos subiit cruciatus, in corpore378

and most painful sufferings in his body;379 was crucified, and died;380 was buried, and remained under the power of death, yet saw no corruption.381 On the third day he arose from the dead,382 with the same body in which he suffered;383 with which also he ascended into heaven, and there sitteth at the right hand of his Father,384 making intercession;385 and shall return to judge men and angels at the end of the world.386

vero perpessiones quam maxime dolorificas; crucifixus est, ac mortuus;387 sepultus est, mansitque sub mortis potestate; nec tamen ullam vidit corruptionem.388 Tertio die surrexit a mortuis,389 cum eodem in quo passus fuerat corpore,390 cum quo etiam ascendit in cœlum, ibique sedens ad dextram Patris391 intercedit,392 rediturus inde in consummatione mundi, ad homines angelosque judicandum.393

V. The Lord Jesus, by his perfect obedience and sacrifice of himself, which he through the eternal Spirit once offered up unto God, hath fully satisfied the justice of his Father,394 and purchased not only reconciliation, but an everlasting inheritance in the kingdom of heaven, for all those whom the Father hath given unto him.395

V. Dominus Jesus obedientia sua perfecta, suique ipsius sacrificio; quod per æternum Spiritum Deo semel obtulit, justitiæ Patris plene satisfecit,396 ac omnibus ei a Patre datis non modo reconciliationem; verum etiam æternam hæreditatem in regno cœlorum acquisivit.397

VI. Although the work of redemption was not actually wrought by Christ till after his incarnation, yet the virtue, efficacy, and benefits thereof were communicated unto the elect, in all ages successively from the beginning of the world, in and by those promises, types,

VI. Quamvis redemptionis opus non nisi post incarnationem ejus, a Christo quidem actu effectum fuerit, vis tamen ejus, efficacia, et beneficia per omnia iam inde a mundi primordiis elapsa secula electis sunt communicata, in et per promissiones illas, typos, et sacrifica,

and sacrifices, wherein he was revealed, and signified to be the seed of the woman which should bruise the serpent’s head, and the lamb slain from the beginning of the world, being yesterday and today the same and forever.398

quibus revelatum erat et significatum hunc esse semen illud mulieris, quod contriturum erat serpentis caput, agnumque illum mactatum ab initio mundi; ut qui heri ac hodie idem est et in sempiternum.399

VII. Christ, in the work of mediation, acteth according to both natures; by each nature doing that which is proper to itself;400 yet, by reason of the unity of the person, that which is proper to one nature is sometimes, in Scripture, attributed to the person denominated by the other nature.401

VII. Christus in opere Mediatorio agit secundum utramque naturam, id agens per utramvis, quod eidem proprium est,402 nonnunquam tamen fit propter personæ unitatem ut quod uni naturæ proprium est, personæ ab altera natura denominatæ in Scriptura tribuatur.403

VIII. To all those for whom Christ hath purchased redemption he doth certainly and effectually apply and communicate the same;404 making intercession for them,405 and revealing unto them, in and by the Word, the mysteries of salvation;406 effectually persuading them by his Spirit to believe and obey; and governing their hearts by his Word and Spirit;407 overcoming all their enemies by his almighty power and wisdom, in such manner and ways as are most consonant to his wonderful and unsearchable dispensation.408

VIII. Pro quibus Christus redemptionem acquisivit, iis omnibus certo quidem ac efficaciter eam applicat impertitque,409 pro eis intercedens,410 eisque in verbo et per verbum revelans mysterium salutis,411 per Spiritum suum eis ut credere velint ac obedire persuadens efficaciter,412 eorumque gubernans corda verbo suo spirituque; sed et vi sua omnipotenti, ac sapientia debellans omnes eorum hostes, iis autem modis mediisque quæ admirabili et inscrutabili ejus dispensationi sunt maxime consentanea.413

Chapter IX. Of Free-will

I. God hath endued the will of man with that natural liberty, that 414is neither forced nor by any absolute necessity of nature determined to good or evil.415

I. Eam humanæ voluntati naturalem Deus indidit libertatem, ut nec cogatur unquam, neque absoluta ulla naturæ necessitate ad bonum aut malum determinetur.416

II. Man, in his state of innocency, had freedom and power to will and to do that which is good and well-pleasing to God, but yet mutably, so that he might fall from it.417

II. Homo in statu innocentiæ libertatem habuit ac potentiam, quod bonum erat Deoque gratum volendi agendique;418 mutabiliter tamen, ita ut illa potuerit excidere.419

III. Man, by his fall into a state of sin, hath wholly lost all ability of will to any spiritual good accompanying salvation;420 so as a natural man, being altogether averse from that good,421 and dead in sin,422 is not able, by his own strength, to convert himself, or to prepare himself thereunto.423

III. Homo per lapsum suum in statum peccati, potentiam omnem quam habuerat voluntas ejus ad bonum aliquod spirituals et saluti contiguum amisit penitus;424 adeo sane ut naturalis homo, utpote ab ejusmodi bono abhorrens prorsus,425 ac in peccato mortuus,426 non possit unquam suis ipsius viribus convertere semet, sed ne quidem ad conversionem se vel præparare.427

IV. When God converts a sinner, and translates him into the state of grace, he freeth him from his natural bondage under sin,428 and by his grace alone enables him freely to will and to do that which is spiritually good;429 yet so

IV. Quandocunque Deus convertit ac in statum gratiæ transfert peccatorem, eundem eximit naturali sua sub peccato servitute,430 solaque gratia sua potentem reddit ad spirituale bonum volendum præstandumque;431 ita tamen ut propter

as that, by reason of his remaining corruption, he doth not perfectly, nor only, will that which is good, but doth also will that which is evil.432

manentem adhuc in eo corruptionem, bonum nec perfecte velit; neque id tantummodo, verum etiam quandoque malum.433

V. The will of man is made perfectly and immutably free to good alone, in the state of glory only.434

V. Voluntas humana perfecte ac immutabiliter libera ad bonum solum redditur non nisi in statu gloriæ.435

Chapter X. Of Effectual Calling

I. All those whom God hath predestinated unto life, and those only, he is pleased, in his appointed and accepted time, effectually to call,436 by his Word and Spirit,437 out of that state of sin and death, in which they are by nature, to grace and salvation by Jesus Christ;438 enlightening their minds, spiritually and savingly, to understand the things of God;439 taking away their heart of stone, and giving unto them an heart of flesh;440 renewing their wills, and by his almighty power determining them to that which is good,441 and effectually drawing them to Jesus Christ;442 yet so as they come most freely, being made willing by his grace.443

I. Deus quos ad vitam prædestinavit omnes, eosque solos dignatur per verbum suum et spiritum444 constituto suo acceptoque tempore vocare efficaciter445 e statu illo peccati et mortis in quo sunt natura constituti, ad gratiam ac salutem per Jesum Christum;446 idque mentes eorum illuminando, ut modo spirituali et salutari quæ Dei sunt intelligant;447 tollendo eorum cor lapideum, donandoque eis cor carneum;448 voluntates eorum renovando ac pro potentia sua omnipotente ad bonum determinando,449 et ad Jesum Christum trahendo efficaciter;450 ita tamen ut illi nihilominus liberrime veniant, volentes nempe facti per illius gratiam.451

II. This effectual call is of God’s

II. Efficax hæc vocatio est a sola

free and special grace alone, not from any thing at all foreseen in man;452 who is altogether passive therein, until, being quickened and renewed by the Holy Spirit,453 he is thereby enabled to answer this call, and to embrace the grace offered and conveyed in it.454

Dei gratia:, gratuita illa et speciali; a nulla autem re in homine prævisa;455 qui in hoc negotio se habet omnino passive, donec per spiritum sanctum vivificatus ac renovatus,456 potis inde factus sit vocationi huic respondere, gratiamque inibi oblatam et exhibitam amplexari.457

III. Elect infants, dying in infancy, are regenerated and saved by Christ through the Spirit,458 who worketh when, and where, and how he pleaseth.459 So also are all other elect persons, who are incapable of being outwardly called by the ministry of the Word.460

III. Electi infantes in infantia sua morientes regenerantur salvanturque a Christo per spiritum461 {qui quando et ubi, et quo sibi placuerit modo operator);462 sicut et reliqui electi omnes, quotquot externæ vocationis per ministerium verbi sunt incapaces.463

IV. Others, not elected, although they may be called by the ministry of the Word,464 and may have some common operations of the Spirit,465 yet they never truly come unto466 Christ, and therefore can not be saved:467 much less can men, not professing the Christian religion, be saved in any other way whatsoever, be they never so diligent to frame their lives according to the light of nature and the law of that religion they do profess;468 and to assert and maintain that they may is

IV. Alii autem, qui non electi sunt, ut ut verbi ministerio vocari possint,469 communesque nonnullas operationes Spiritus experiri,470 nunquam tamen vere ad Christum accedunt, proindeque nec salvari possunt.471 Multo quidem minus poterunt illi, quotquot religionem Christianam non profitentur (summam licet operam navaverint moribus suis ad naturæ lumen, istiusque quam profitentur religionis legem componendis), extra hanc unicam viam salutem unquam obtinere.472 Atque huic quidem contrarium

very pernicious, and to be detested.473

statuere ac defendere, perniciosum admodum est ac detestandum.474

Chapter XI. Of Justification

I. Those whom God effectually calleth he also freely justifieth;475 not by infusing righteousness into them, but by pardoning their sins, and by accounting and accepting their persons as righteous: not for any thing wrought in them, or done by them, but for Christ’s sake alone; nor476 by imputing faith itself, the act of believing, or any other evangelical obedience to them, as their righteousness; but by imputing the obedience and satisfaction of Christ unto them,477 they receiving and resting on him and his righteousness by faith; which faith they have not of themselves, it is the gift of God.478

I. Quos Deus vocat efficaciter, eosdem etiam gratis justificat,479 non quidem justitiam iis infundendo, sed eorum peccata condonando, personasque pro justis reputando atque acceptando; neque id certe propter quicquam aut in iis productum, aut ab iis præstitum, verum Christi solius ergo; eisque ad justitiam non fidem ipsam, non credendi actum, aut aliam quamcunque obedientiam evangelicam, verum obedientiam ac satisfactionem Christi imputando,480 eum nempe recipientibus, eique ac justitiæ ejus per fidem innitentibus; quam illi fidem ex dono Dei, non a seipsis, habent.481

II. Faith, thus receiving and resting on Christ and his righteousness, is the alone instrument of justification;482 yet is it not alone in the person justified, but is ever accompanied with all other saving graces, and is no dead faith, but worketh by love.483

II. Fides hoc modo Christum recipiens, eique innitens ac justitiæ ejus, est justificationis unicum instrumentum;484 in homine tamen justificato hæc non est solitaria, verum gratiis aliis omnibus salutaribus semper comitata; neque est hæc fides mortua, sed quæ per charitatem operatur.485

III. Christ, by his obedience and

III. Qui hunc in modum justificantur,

death, did fully discharge the debt of all those that are thus justified, and did make a proper, real, and full satisfaction to his Father’s justice in their behalf.486 Yet inasmuch as he was given by the Father for them,487 and his obedience and satisfaction accepted in their stead,488 and both freely, not for any thing in them, their justification is only of free grace;489 that both the exact justice and rich grace of God might be glorified in the justification of sinners.490

eorum omnium debita Christus per obedientiam suam mortemque prorsus dissolvit; eorumque vice justitiæ Patris sui realem, plenum, et proprie dictam satisfactionem præstitit.491 Quum tamen non propter in iis quicquam, verum gratuito Pater cum Christum ipsum pro eis dederit,492 tum obedientiam ejus ac satisfactionem tanquam eorum loco constituti493 acceptaverit; omnino a gratia gratuita est eorum justificatio;494 Quo nimirum Dei tum accurata justitia tum locuples gratia glorificata foret in justificatione peccatorum.495

IV. God did, from all eternity, decree to justify all the elect,496 and Christ did, in the fullness of time, die for their sins, and rise again for their justification:497 nevertheless, they are not justified until the Holy Spirit doth, in due time, actually apply Christ unto them.498

IV. Ab æterno decrevit Deus electos omnes justificare,499 Christusque in temporis plenitudine mortuus est pro eorum peccatis, et in justificationem eorum resurrexit:500 nihilo minus tamen justificati prius non sunt, quam Christum eis in tempore suo opportuno Spiritus Sanctus actu applicuerit.501

V. God doth continue to forgive the sins of those that are justified;502 and although they can never fall from the state of justification,503 yet they may by their sins fall under God’s fatherly displeasure, and not have the light of his countenance restored unto them, until they humble themselves,

V. Perseverat Deus eorum peccata condonare quos semel justificavit,504 quin et etiamsi excidere statu justificationis nunquam possint;505 fieri tamen potest ut iræ Dei, paternæ quidem illi, per peccata sua se exponant, nec lumen paterni vultus prius sibi habeant restitutum, quam semet ipsos humiliaverint,

confess their sins, beg pardon, and renew their faith and repentance.506

peccata agnoverint, imploraverint veniam, fidem denique et pœnitentiam suam renovaverint.507

VI. The justification of believers under the Old Testament was, in all these respects, one and the same with the justification of believers under the New Testament.508

VI. Justificatio fidelium sub Vetere ac Novo509 Testamento quoad isthæc omnia est una eademque.510

Chapter XII. Of Adoption

All those that are justified God vouchsafeth, in and for his only Son Jesus Christ, to make partakers of the grace of adoption;511 by which they are taken into the number, and enjoy the liberties and privileges of the children of God;512 have his name put upon them;513 receive the Spirit of adoption;514 have access to the throne of grace with boldness;515 are enabled to cry, Abba, Father;516 are pitied,517 protected,518 provided for,519 and chastened by him as by a father;520 yet never cast off,521 but sealed to the day of redemption,522 and inherit the promises,523 as heirs of everlasting salvation.524

Deus justificatos omnes dignatur in filio suo unigenito Jesu Christo, et propter eundem participes facere gratiæ Adoptionis;525 per quam in numerum filiorum Dei assumuntur, taliumque immunitatibus ac privilegiis potiuntur,526 impositum sibi habent nomen Dei,527 Spiritum adoptionis accipiunt,528 aditum habent ad thronum gratiæ cum confidentia,529 potestatem consequuntur clamandi Abba Pater,530 commiserationem,531 tutelam,532 et providentiam533 sortiuntur; quin et castigationem Dei paternam experiuntur;534 nunquam tamen abdicantur,535 verum in diem redemptionis consignati536 promissiones obtinent hæreditario jure,537 ut qui hæredes sunt æternæ salutis.538

Chapter XIII. Of Sanctification

I. They who are effectually called and regenerated, having a new heart and a new spirit created in them, are further sanctified, really and personally, through the virtue of Christ’s death and resurrection,539 by his Word and Spirit dwelling in them;540 the dominion of the whole body of sin is destroyed,541 and the several lusts thereof are more and more weakened and mortified,542and they more and more quickened and strengthened, in all saving graces,543 to the practice of true holiness, without which no man shall see the Lord.544

I. Quotquot efficaciter vocantur, ac regenerantur, cor novum habentes novumque spiritum in se creatum, sunt virtute mortis et resurrectionis Christi545 per verbum ejus spiritumque in eis inhabitantem546 ulterius sandificati, realiter quidem ac personaliter: totius corporis peccati dominium in eos destruitur,547 ejusque variæ libidines debilitantur indies magis magisque ac mortificantur;548 illi interim magis magisque in omni gratia salutari vivificantur et corroborantur indies,549 ad praxim veræ sanctimoniæ, qua quidem destitutus nemo unquam videbit Dominum.550

II. This sanctification is throughout in the whole man,551 yet imperfect in this life; there abideth still some remnants of corruption in every part,552 whence ariseth a continual and irreconcilable war, the flesh lusting against the spirit, and the spirit against the flesh.553

II. Universalis est hæc et per totum hominem diffusa sanctificatio,554 verum in hac vita est imperfecta nonnullis corruptionis reliquiis adhuc in omni parte remanentibus,555 unde bellum exoritur perpetuum et implacabile; hinc carne adversus spiritum, illinc spiritu adversus carnem concupiscente.556

III. In which war, although the remaining corruption for a time may much prevail,557 yet, through the continual supply of strength from the sanctifying Spirit of

III. In quo quidem bello licet corruptio residua possit aliquandiu prævalere plurimum,558 pars tamen regenita, sanctificante Christi spiritu perpetuas ferente suppetias,

Christ, the regenerate part doth overcome;559 and so the saints grow in grace,560 perfecting holiness in the fear of God.561

evadit victrix);562 adeoque sancti in gratia crescunt,563 sanctitatem in timore Domini perficientes.564

Chapter XIV. Of Saving Faith

I. The grace of faith, whereby the elect are enabled to believe to the saving of their souls,565 is the work of the Spirit of Christ in their hearts,566 and is ordinarily wrought by the ministry of the Word;567 by which also, and by the administration of the sacraments and prayer, it is increased and strengthened.568

I. Gratia Fidei, qua electi credere valent ad animarum suarum salutem,569 Spiritus Christi opus est in eorum cordibus operantis,570 effectum plerumque verbi Dei ministerio,571 quo eodem etiam, ut et administratione Sacramentorum atque oratione robur ei accedit ac incrementum.572

II. By this faith a Christian believeth to be true whatsoever is revealed in the Word, for the authority of God himself speaking therein;573 and acteth differently upon that which each particular passage thereof containeth; yielding obedience to the commands,574 trembling at the threatenings,575 and embracing the promises of God for this life and that which is to come.576 But the principal acts of saving faith are accepting, receiving, and resting upon Christ alone

II. Hac Fide credit Christianus verum esse quicquid in verbo revelatur, propter authoritatem ipsius inibi loquentis Dei;577 et varie quidem in illud agit tum obsequendo mandatis,578 tum ad minas contremiscens,579 tum etiam promissa Dei, seu præsentem hanc vitam seu futuram spectent, amplexando,580 pro varia nempe ratione illarum rerum, quæ in singulis verbi partibus continentur. Verum fidei salvificæ actus illi sunt præcipui, Christi acceptatio et receptio, in eumque solum

for justification, sanctification, and eternal life, by virtue of the covenant of grace.581

recumbentia pro justificatione, sanctificatione, ipsaque adeo vita æterna, virtute fœderis gratiæ consequendis.582

III. This faith is different in degrees, weak or strong;583 may be often and many ways assailed and weakened, but gets the victory;584 growing up in many to the attainment of a full assurance through Christ,585 who is both the author and finisher of our faith.586

III. Fides hæc pro diversis ejus gradibus debilior est aut fortior;587 impugnari quidem sæpenumero multisque modis ac debilitari potest, non ita tamen quin victrix evadat;588 et quidem in multis ad plenum usque certitudinem per Christum adolescit,589 qui fidei nostræ idem author est et consummator.590

Chapter XV. Of Repentance unto Life

I. Repentance unto life is an evangelical grace,591 the doctrine whereof is to be preached by every minister of the gospel, as well as that of faith in Christ.592

I. Resipiscentia ad vitam est gratia Evangelica,593 cuius quidem doctrina pariter ac illa de fide in Christum est a singulis ministris Evangelii prædicanda.

II. By it a sinner, out of the sight and sense, not only of the danger, but also of the filthiness and odiousness of his sins, as contrary to the holy nature and righteous law of God, and upon the apprehension of his mercy in Christ to such as are penitent, so grieves for and hates his sins as to turn from them all unto God,594 purposing and endeavoring

II. Per eam peccator ex inspectu sensuque non solum periculi verum etiam turpitudinis, ac naturæ peccatorum suorum prorsus abominandæ.595 utpote sanctæ Dei naturæ, justæque legi adversantium, atque e perspecta ejus erga pœnitentes in Christo misericordia, ita peccata sua deflet ac detestatur, ut ab eis omnibus ad Deum convertatur596 cum proposito conatuque in cunctis mandatorum

to walk with him in all the ways of his commandments.597

ejus viis cum eodem ambulandi.598

III. Although repentance be not to be rested in as any satisfaction for sin, or any cause of the pardon thereof,599 which is the act of God’s free grace in Christ;600 yet is it of such necessity to all sinners that none may expect pardon without it.601

III. Etsi resipiscentiæ nobis fidendum non sit, ac si ea esset ulla aut pro peccatis satisfactio, aut causa remissionis peccatorum602 (qui gratiæ Dei in Christo gratuitæ actus est),603 est nihilominus cunctis peccatoribus usque adeo necessaria, ut sine ea nulla cuivis unquam remissio sit expectanda.604

IV. As there is no sin so small but it deserves damnation,605 so there is no sin so great that it can bring damnation upon those who truly repent.606

IV. Quemadmodum nullum est peccatum adeo exiguum ut damnationem non mereatur,607 ita neque magnum adeo peccatum ullum est, ut damnationem inferre possit vere pœnitentibus.608

V. Men ought not to content themselves with a general repentance, but it is every man’s duty to endeavor to repent of his particular sins particularly.609

V. In resipiscentia generali acquiescendum non est, verum ad id contendere tenetur quisque, ut singulorum suorum peccatorum quam particularem agat pœnitentiam.610

VI. As every man is bound to make private confession of his sins to God, praying for the pardon thereof,611 upon which, and the forsaking of them, he shall find mercy;612 so he that scandalizeth his brother, or the Church of Christ, ought to be willing, by a private or public confession and sorrow for his sin, to declare his repentance

VI. Quemadmodum autem tenetur quivis peccata sua Deo privatim confiteri, et pro remissione illorum precibus contendere:613 (quod si præstiterit et peccata simul dereliquerit, misericordiam consequetur)614 ita qui fratri suo, aut Ecclesiæ Christi, scandalo fuerit, promptus et paratus esse debet qua confessione sive privata, sive etiam publica, qua de peccatis

to those that are offended,615 who are thereupon to be reconciled to him, and in love to receive him.616

suis dolore, resipiscentiam suam eis quibus offendiculo fuerit declarare,617 quo præstito illi redire cum eo in gratiam debent, eumque denuo cum charitate recipere.618

Chapter XVI. Of Good Works

I. Good works are only such as God hath commanded in his holy Word,619 and not such as, without the warrant thereof, are devised by men out of blind zeal, or upon any pretense of good intention.620

I. Bona opera ea tantum sunt quæ in verbo suo sancto præcepit Deus;621 minime autem ea quæ absque ulla illius authoritate, sunt ab hominibus excogitata, sive e cæco zelo id factum fuerit, seu bonæ intentionis prætextu quoviscunque.622

II. These good works, done in obedience to God’s commandments, are the fruits and evidences of a true and lively faith;623 and by them believers manifest their thankfulness,624 strengthen their assurance,625 edify their brethren,626 adorn the profession of the gospel,627 stop the mouths of the adversaries,628 and glorify God,629 whose workmanship they are, created in Christ Jesus thereunto,630 that, having their fruit unto holiness, they may have the end, eternal life.631

II. Bona hæc opera e conscientia mandatorum Dei præstita vivæ veræque fidei fructus sunt ac evidentiæ;632 per hæc fideles gratitudinem suam manifestant,633 de salute certitudinem suam augent,634 fratres suos ædificant,635 Evangelii professionem ornant,636 obturant ora adversantibus,637 ac Deum denique glorificant,638 cuius opificium sunt in Jesu Christo ad hæc creati,639 quo fructum habentes ad sanctimoniam, finem consequantur æternam vitam.640

III. Their ability to do good

III. Quod bonis operibus idonei

works is not at all of themselves, but wholly from the Spirit of Christ.641 And that they may be enabled thereunto, besides the graces they have already received, there is required an actual influence of the same Holy Spirit to work in them to will and to do of his good pleasure;642 yet are they not hereupon to grow negligent, as if they were not bound to perform any duty unless upon a special motion of the Spirit; but they ought to be diligent in stirring up the grace of God that is in them.643

sint præstandis omnino id a spiritu Christi est, nullatenus autem e seipsis.644 Et quo eis præstandis pares fiant, prater habitus gratiæ iam infusos, ejusdem Spiritus sancti actualis porro requiritur influentia, qua nempe in iis operetur tum velle tum etiam efficere pro suo ipsius beneplacito:645 sed neque tamen iis proinde socordiæ sese licet permittere; ac si nisi specialiter eos excitante Spiritu ad nulla pietatis officia præstanda tenerentur; verum sedulam debent navare operam sustitandæ illi quæ in iis est divinæ gratiæ.646

IV. They who in their obedience attain to the greatest height which is possible in this life, are so far from being able to supererogate and to do more than God requires, as647 that they fall short of much which in duty they are bound to do.648

IV. Qui gradum obedientiæ summum quidem in hac vita possibilem assequuntur, tantum abest ut supererogare quicquam possint ac plus præstare quam quod Deus requisiverit, ut multum sane subsidant infra illud, quod ex officio præstare obligantur.649

V. We can not, by our best works, merit pardon of sin, or eternal life at the hand of God, by reason of the great disproportion that is between them and the glory to come, and the infinite distance that is between us and God, whom by them we can neither profit nor satisfy

V. Peccatorum veniam, aut vitam æternam de Deo mereri non valemus, ne optimis quidem operibus nostris; cum propter summam illam inter ea et futuram gloriam disparitatem; tum etiam propter infinitam distantiam quæ inter nos ac Deum intercedit; cui nos per illa nec prodesse

for the debt of our former sins;650 but when we have done all we can, we have done but our duty, and are unprofitable servants;651 and because, as they are good, they proceed from his Spirit;652 and as they are wrought by us, they are defiled and mixed with so much weakness and imperfection that they can not endure the severity of God’s judgment.653

quicquam possumus, neque pro antecedentium peccatorum nostrorum debito satisfacere;654 verum cum quantum possumus fecerimus, non nisi quod debemus præstiterimus, ac servi inutiles futuri sumus;655 tum denique quoniam a spiritu Dei in quantum bona sunt proficiscuntur,656 ita vero sunt coinquinata, tantumque imperfectionis ac infirmitas admistum habent, prout a nobis efficiuntur, ut strictum Dei judicium non sint ferendo.657

VI. Yet notwithstanding, the persons of believers being accepted through Christ, their good works also are accepted in him,658 not as though they were in this life wholly unblamable and unreprovable in God’s sight;659 but that he, looking upon them in his Son, is pleased to accept and reward that which is sincere, although accompanied with many weaknesses and imperfections.660

VI. Nihilominus tamen acceptis in gratiam per Christum fidelium personis, eorum etiam opera bona per eundem accepta sunt;661 non quod in hac vita sint omnis culpæ prorsus immunia, quæque in conspectu Dei nullam reprehensionem mereantur;662 verum quod illa respiciens in filio suo Deus, quod sincerum est, utcunque multis infirmitatibus ac imperfectionibus involutum, acceptare dignetur ac remunerari.663

VII. Works done by unregenerate men, although for the matter of them they may be things which God commands, and of good use both to themselves and others;664

VII. Opera nondum regenitorum, licet, quoad materiam præcepto divino conformia esse possint, sibique ipsis et aliis item utilia;665 cum tamen neque a corde profluant per fidem

yet because they proceed not from a heart purified by faith,666 nor are done in a right manner, according to the Word,667 nor to a right end, the glory of God;668 they are therefore sinful, and can not please God, or make a man meet to receive grace from God.669 And yet their neglect of them is more sinful and displeasing unto God.670

depurato,671 nec secundum verbum eo quo par est præstentur modo,672 sed neque ad finem debitum, Dei nempe gloriam, destinentur;673 sunt proinde peccata, nec Deo grata esse possunt, nec reddere quenquam valent idoneum ad gratiam a Deo recipiendum.674 Ejusmodi tamen operum neglectu, gravius quidem illi peccant Deumque offendunt vehementius.675

Chapter XVII. Of the Perseverance of the Saints

I. They whom God hath accepted in his Beloved, effectually called and sanctified by his Spirit, can neither totally nor finally fall away from the state of grace; but shall certainly persevere therein to the end, and be eternally saved.676

I. Quotquot Deus in dilecto suo acceptavit, vocavit efficaciter ac per Spiritum suum sanctificavit, non possunt illi statu gratiæ aut finaliter excidere aut totaliter; verum in eo ad finem usque certo perseverabunt, ac salutem æternam consequentur.677

II. This perseverance of the saints depends, not upon their own freewill, but upon the immutability of the decree of election, flowing from the free and unchangeable love of God the Father;678 upon the efficacy of the merit and intercession of Jesus Christ;679 the abiding

II. hæc autem sanctorum perseverantia, non pendet a libero ipsorum arbitrio, verum a decreti electionis immutabilitate (quod ex amore Dei Patris fluxit, gratuito illo ac immutabili),680 a meriti Jesu Christi ac intercessionis efficacia,681 a Spiritus et seminis Dei in iis permansione;682

of the Spirit and of the seed of God within them;683 and the nature of the covenant of grace:684 from all which ariseth also the certainty and infallibility thereof.685

a natura denique fœderis gratiæ;686 e quibus omnibus etiam emergit certitudo ejusdem et infallibilitas.687

III. Nevertheless they may, through the temptations of Satan and of the world, the prevalency of corruption remaining in them, and the neglect of the means of their preservation, fall into grievous sins;688 and for a time continue therein:689 whereby they incur God’s displeasure,690 and grieve his Holy Spirit;691 come to be deprived of some measure of their graces and comforts;692 have their hearts hardened,693 and their consciences wounded;694 hurt and scandalize others,695 and bring temporal judgments upon themselves.696

III. Nihilo tamen minus fieri potest ut iidem illi, qua Satanæ mundique tentatione, qua manentis adhuc in iis corruptionis prævalentia, et neglectu mediorum conservationis suæ, in peccata gravia incidant,697 in eisque ad tempus commorentur;698 unde iram Dei sibi ipsis contrahunt,699; ejusque Spiritum Sanctum contristant,700 gratias suas et consolationes quadantenus et quoad gradus nonnullos amittunt,701 corda sibi habent indurata,702 et vulneratas conscientias;703 aliis nocumento sunt et offendiculo,704 sibimet ipsis denique accersunt judicia Dei temporalia.705

Chapter XVIII. Of the Assurance of Grace and Salvation

I. Although hypocrites and other unregenerate men may vainly deceive themselves with false hopes and carnal presumptions of being in the favor of God and estate of salvation,706 which hope of theirs

I. Quamvis fieri potest ut hypocritæ aliique homines non regeniti spe vana falsisque (pro corruptæ naturæ more) opinionibus præsumptis, se decipiant, favorem Dei, statumque salutis sibi falso arrogantes;707 quæ illorum

shall perish:708 yet such as truly believe in the Lord Jesus, and love him in sincerity, endeavoring to walk in all good conscience before him, may in this life be certainly assured that they are in a state of grace,709 and may rejoice in the hope of the glory of God, which hope shall never make them ashamed.710

spes peribit:711 qui tamen in Dominum Jesum vere credunt, eumque sincere diligunt, studentes coram ipso in omni bona conscientia ambulare; evadere possunt in hac vita certi se in statu gratiæ esse constitutos;712 quin etiam lætari possunt spe gloriæ Dei, quæ quidem spes nunquam eos pudefaciet.713

II. This certainty is not a bare conjectural and probable persuasion, grounded upon a fallible hope;714 but an infallible assurance of faith, founded upon the divine truth of the promises of salvation,715 the inward evidence of those graces unto which these promises are made,716 the testimony of the Spirit of adoption witnessing with our spirits that we are the children of God:717 which Spirit is the earnest of our inheritance, whereby we are sealed to the day of redemption.718

II. Hæc certitudo non est persuasio mere conjecturalis et probabilis, innixa spe fallaci;719 verum infallibilis quædam fidei certitudo, fundamentum habens divinam promissionum salutis veritatem;720 gratiarum, quibus promissiones illæ fiunt internam evidentiam;721 testimonium denique spiritus adoptionis una cum spiritibus nostris testificantis nos esse filios Dei;722 qui quidem spiritus arrhabo est hæreditatis nostræ, quo in diem redemtionis sigillamur.723

III. This infallible assurance doth not so belong to the essence of faith, but that a true believer may wait long, and conflict with many difficulties before he be partaker of it:724 yet, being enabled by the Spirit to know the things which are freely given him of God, he

III. Hæc certitudo infallibilis, non ita spectat essentiam fidei, quin vere fidelis expectare quandoque diutius, et cum variis difficultatibus confligere prius possit, quam illius compos fiat,725 verum poterit idem ordinariorum usu debito mediorum, absque revelatione ulla extraordinaria

may, without extraordinary revelation, in the right use of ordinary means, attain thereunto.726 And therefore it is the duty of every one to give all diligence to make his calling and election sure;727 that thereby his heart may be enlarged in peace and joy in the Holy Ghost, in love and thankfulness to God, and in strength and cheerfulness in the duties of obedience, the proper fruits of this assurance:728 so far is it from inclining men to looseness.729

eam adipisci,730 spiritu nempe quæ Deus illi gratuito donaverit cognoscendi facultatem subministrante. Proindeque tenetur quisque, quo vocationem suam sibi et electionem certmn faciat, omnem adhibere diligentiam,731 unde cor suum habeat pace et gaudio in spiritu sancto, in Deum amore et gratitudine, in actibus observantiæ robore et alacritate dilatatum; qui certitudinus huius fructus proprii sunt ac genuini.732 Tantum abest ut homines inde ad omnem nequitiam discingantur.733

IV. True believers may have the assurance of their salvation divers ways shaken, diminished, and intermitted; as, by negligence in preserving of it; by falling into some special sin, which woundeth, the conscience, and grieveth the Spirit; by some sudden or vehement temptation; by God’s withdrawing the light of his countenance, and suffering even such as fear him to walk in darkness and to have no light:734 yet are they never utterly destitute of that seed of God, and life of faith, that love of Christ and the brethren, that sincerity of heart and conscience of duty, out

IV. Certitudo salutis vere fidelibus multifariam concuti potest et imminui imo et quandoque interrumpi; conservandi scilicet eam incuria; lapsu in peccatum aliquod insigne, quod conscientiam vulnerat, spiritumque contristat; tentatione aliqua vehementi ac subitanea; uti etiam Deo vultus sui lumen subducente, ac permittente ut vel illi qui ipsum timent in tenebris ambulent omni prorsus lumine viduati:735 nunquam tamen destituuntur penitus illo Dei semine vitaque fidei. Christi illa fratrumque dilectione, ea sinceritate cordis et pietatis officia præstandi conscientia; unde per

of which, by the operation of the Spirit, this assurance may in due time be revived,736 and by the which, in the mean time, they are supported from utter despair.737

operationem spiritus eadem illa certitudo tempestive possit reviviscere:738 quibusque interim ne prorsus in desperationem ruant suffulciuntur.739

Chapter XIX. Of the Law of God

I. God gave to Adam a law, as a covenant of works, by which he bound him and all his posterity to personal, entire, exact, and perpetual obedience; promised life upon the fulfilling, and threatened death upon the breach of it; and endued him with power and ability to keep it.740

I. Deus Adamo legem dedit ut fœdus operum, quo cum illum ipsum tum posteros ejus omnes, ad obedientiam personalem, integram, exquisitam simul et perpetuam obligavit, pollicitus vitam si observarent, violatoribus autem mortem interminatus; eundemque potentia et viribus imbuit, quibus par esset illam observando.741

II. This law, after his fall, continued to be a perfect rule of righteousness; and, as such, was delivered by God upon mount Sinai in ten commandments, and written in two tables;742 the first four commandments containing our duty towards God, and the other six our duty to man.743

II. Lex ista post lapsum non desiit esse justitiæ regula perfectissima; quo etiam nomine a Deo est in monte Sinai tradita, tabulis duabus descripta, decem præceptis comprehensa;744 quorum quatuor prima officium nostrum erga Deum, sex autem reliqua nostrum erga homines officium complectuntur.745

III. Beside this law, commonly called moral, God was pleased to give to the people of Israel, as a Church under age, ceremonial laws, containing several typical ordinances,

III. Præter autem hanc legem, quæ moralis vulgo audit, visum est Deo ut populo Israelitico tanquam Ecclesiæ minorenni leges daret ceremoniales instituta typica multifaria

partly of worship, prefiguring Christ, his graces, actions, sufferings, and benefits;746 and partly holding forth divers instructions of moral duties.747 All which ceremonial laws are now abrogated under the New Testament.748

continentes; partim de cultu, Christi gratias, actiones, perpessiones ac beneficia præfigurantia;749 partim autem de moralibus officiis institutiones varias exhibentia.750 Quæ leges ceremoniales omnes hodie sub novo instrumento sunt abrogatæ.751

IV. To them also, as a body politic, he gave sundry judicial laws, which expired together with the state of that people, not obliging any other, now, further than the general equity thereof may require.752

IV. Iisdem etiam tanquam corpori politico leges multas dedit judiciales, quæ una cum istius populi politeia expirarunt, nullos hodie alios obligantes supra quod generalis et communis earum æquitas postularit.753

V.
